THE HAKBOCR NUISANCE. 
Poconos enrnnneee, 

The action’ of the Hon. Captain Barnes- 
Lawrence, K.N,, in severely reprimanding the} - 
masters of several launches for the intolerabia 
nuisance caused by the excessive and unneces- 
sary use of their steam whistles will be univer- 
sally endorsed. From early morn till dewy 
eve the harbour resounds with the_ear-racking, 
nerve-destroying fulminations ‘of vagabond 
Jaunches: Even in the dead of night, when one 
might be inclined to think thatthe arabs of the 
hay could proceed without a contioual varia- 
tion of clarhant discordancy, the hooting and 
shrieking of the whistlescontindcunabated. The 
masters of the steam Jaunches seem to take 6 


they have cultivated the art so assiduausly that 
they can ran up the whole gamut of discord’ 
without a sing! repetition. Duriog the busy 
period: of the day, the whistling is partly. 
drowned by the hum of the city, but afterwards 
it is painfu'ly patent. We all had the idea that, 
there was some illegality in the perpetual clam- 
our created by the launches, but -we suffered 
in sitence, although there must have been many 
‘an uncanonical sound stifled ia private. Were 
it necessary tht the launclies should be 
continually emitting these raucous noises we 
might possibly endure them,, but when we 
know thal they are needless and unlawful the 
pain is all the more intense. Fortunately, the 
Harbour ‘authorities have 1 en the matter in 
hand, and’ inaugurated A Gaonpaign againat ‘the 
disturbers of the peace. The mastess of three 
launches were charged’ before the Harbour 
Master yesterday with " untawfylly using the 
steam whistles” of their launches: One‘of the 
defendants, it was stated, gave a couple of 
«blasts when one onty was necessa'y; another 
eeated) his so-called signals out of pure joy- 
ousness/‘of spirit; while the third man blew 
“long -blasts"” without thyme or reason. 
Anyone who has stoud on Blake Pier in the 
evening must have been struck by the happy 
hecdlessness with which the masters of faunches 
give vent to their feelings, through the medium 
of the steam whisiles. ‘They curse and swear 
at each other in the most atrocious fashion, and 
ifthere is no other launch or sampan in sight 
they deride the horizon, That sort of thing 
should be put down wilh a firm hand... The 
three men who came before the Harbour-matter 
yesterday may consider themselves lucky !n: 
getting off so cheaply as they did. Captain 
Barnes-Lawrence will have the support of the’ 
community ‘should he deal sternly with those 
who contravenc the law by making night and 
-day-hideous. with their sieam whistles, itisto 
be hoped, however, that the owners and masters 
of steam launches will be informed of the Har:, 
bour-maste:'s remarks, and abide in fotare. by 
the Istter of tho Jar. Le 25

Thesroasting thirst which Indian policeman |" 
No. 878 had yesterday landed him before Mr. 
F. A. Hazeland'this morning, He was dn duty 
oa the Peak Road yesterday when he saw.a 
coolie coming ia his diuection with (wo haskets 
of. mineral waters slung across his shoulder on 
a bamboo pole. He could hold his thirst no 
longer so he app.oached thecoolie and begged 
for a bottle of soda watcr. The coolie replied 
that the minerals were not his property ; if they 
were the officer was welcome to the whole 
basket; and he proceeded _on-his way. The 
policeman went after the cudlie, snatched the 
order-book oui of the basket aod banged the 
coolie over'the head with it, knocking him 
down, Naturally'the basket of minerals fell to} 
the ground tooand nine bottles of soda exploded. 
The Indian went on his way and the coolie 
returned to “Blue Bungalow,” the Peak, and 
reported the matter to his mistress, giving the 
number of the policeman, The mistress’ wrote 
to the Captain Superintendent of Police about 
the’ matter and the policeman’ was charged. 
He was placed before Mr, F. A. Hazeland this 
morning, by ‘Inspectom Warnock, on a charge 
of-assault The defendant, of course, denied 
the charge; he did not know the covlie, naver 
saw him‘during his residence, in the Colony, 
and such Jike excuses, but the Court held him 
guilty of the cha ge. He was made to pay a 
‘fine-of $25, and the coolie instsucted that in 
cuse the policemap give-him any trouble in 
the fulure he was to complain to the police,

THE BILL POSTING NUISANCE, 
————_———— 
POLICK ON THE WARPATH, 
1 o_o ‘ . 

Inspector Smith placed twa Chinamen before 
Mr. F, A._Hazeland, at the Magistracy, thia 
morning, on a charge of affixing posters on the 
walls of the old harbour office, early this morn- 
ing, without the consent of the Goveroment, 
The ‘manner in which the defendants’ were 
arrested ‘was very amusing. The police say 
thet the bill-posters,start work very early in the 
‘morning and ‘eave traces ‘of. their visits 
even on walls thal hear: the sign, " Post No 
They slart atan early hour simply 
because they: think there is no one about to, 
interfere with them. This morning, while an- 
Indian policeman was passing the old harbour 
office, he. noticed a coolie on the top of a ladder 
in the act of posting an immense ‘Japanese 
poster, while a smail boy was.tookiag on with 
another bundle under his arm. When he 
saw the police he cleared, but the man 
on the top of the ‘ladder was too busy ta 
see that. The policeman approached the 
ladder and told the bill- “poster to come down. 
He deciined, The policeman polled away the 
ladder from. under the Chinaman and teft him 
hanging by his hands on the ledge of the wall: 
while the brush which he held in one hand for 
sticking the poster, dropped on the upluraed 
face of the pol:ceman, leaving his face covered 
with paste. ,-The Chinaman, was then dragged 
down by the legs. The bill posting nuisance 
not‘only annoys the public but the potice come 
in forasharcof jt. Some time last’ week a 
huge“poster was affixed 1-1 the-notice buard o1 
No. 5 Police Station covering important notices 
onthe board, The defendants pleaded guilty, 
-soying that they did not know the law. His 
Worship fined the bill-poster $5, and the little 
boy, who toak to histheels when be saw the 


policeman, was discharged with a caution,

ACCELERATIHD MAIE SERVICE, 
arp (tith August.) ; 

It is nsmall world, this terrestrial globe on 
which we live, after all,'and every day some 
new ‘departure bears evidence of its being 
made smaller by reduction of distances and 
times in traversing them | ‘That being so 
our readers will na doubt peruse, with as much 
interest as they will appreciate the importance 
of, the fast service to be. instituted between 
Hongkong and England vfa Canada, by which 
passengers and mails will reach England on 
the ‘29th ‘day out from Hongkong—a matter 
of great consideration to those compelled to 
“run home”! on urgent private affairs, but who 
are only able to obtain very stort leave, as well 
asto those who cannot leave their business 
in the Colony for longer than can be av .ided. 
“Home via'Cannda" has long been a house- 
hold phrase, but it bids. fair to become quite a 
matter'of course that Canada will be the route 
for home-returning Fur-Easterners, with thes 


alternative British route offered them, «and: 


which ts certainly more enjoy ible than that vfa 
Suez. Therefore thé fact shonid’be of wide 
interest, not anly in this ‘Colony but in China 
in general, that Mr, J. Craddock, acting agent 
of the Canadian Fatific Railway Company, 

fice 
at Mantreal announcing ‘the fact that ag 
accelerated mail ‘service between Hongkong 
and England has been arranged with the 


Batish Postal Aathorities. Under this new 


fast service the Aepress steamers will for the 


-prevent leave Hongkong at 4 pm, on Thursdays 


instead ¢f noon on. Wednesdays, as heretofore, 
The fi st sfeamer to Jeave Hongkong under the 
new arrangement will be the Mmpress of India 
siting at 4 pam. on the 3 th instant, to be 
followed by the Zorteess af Jatan on the 27h 
September, and the Empress of Chinavon the 
2sih October; subsequent sailings to be 
announced ater. - he time in transit between 
this ard Yokohama is also being consider> 
ably reduced—the run, including stops at: 
Shanghai, Nagasaki and Kobe, berg accoin- 
plished in well under seven: days. . The 
schedule time from Hongkong to Vancouver 1s 
under 18 days; and the whole trip through to 
England will be‘accomplished in about 29 days. 


arrival of the impress boats at Vancouver by 
which mails and passengers will be carried to 
Quebec, there joining one of the fine new t- 


and 7 mpress of refund, 14,500 tons register) 
and from there across to Liverpool in some- 
thing under seven days. The St. Law- 
rence River route has, apart from its’ na- 
tural beauties, the advantage of consider- 
ably Jessening the actual ocean voyage, and 
already the new Atlantic Amfres es have 
established a nume for their excellént steadi- 
ness and general sea-going qualities. On 
the outward voyage the first steamer: to 
leave Vancouver, under the new faust service, 
will be the Huspress af Ch ms, sailing thence 
onthe 4th September—passengers and mails for 
her having left Liverpool by the Zsupress cf 
ireland on th: 2ith August. The. Zatpress of 
Caina is due at Yokohama on the 10th Sep- 
tember and Hongkong on the 23rd Sep- 
tember This sading will be followed by 
the Aunpress of [adia and Fempress af Japan 


‘atintervals of. 28 days, and subsequent de- 


pajures will be announced in due course, 
the enterprise of-the Canadian Pacific k nil- 
toad Company is worthy of congratulation, 
and, whit is more to the purpose, of every 
possible support and encouragement. © : 


CANION-HANKOW RAILWAY 
DIFFICULTIES. 
: f 
(13th August.) | 
It is with unallayed satisfaction - and 
hopefulness that. we annoitnce the fact 
that work in connection 
struction of the Canton-Hankow Railway 
was resumed, with” every. appeararice of 


energy and determination to forge fahead at’ 


the utmost speed compatible with safety and 
solid ‘workmanship, on Saturday. So long 
have the squabbles, jealousies and petty 
grievances delayed the operations that it 
seemed possible the completion of the line 
might be postponed for another couple of 
years, ora decade, or perhaps indefinitely. Jt 
ms ages sincé the line was. in the hands 
of a foreign syndicate ; it is certainly a con- 
siderable time ago since, it camie under the 


See 


control of the Chinese, mainly through the’ 


disinterested and magnanimous loan granted 
by the Briush Government, through the me- 
dium of the Hongkong and Shanghai Bank- 
ing Corporation; and it is cerlainly many 
months since thé Chinese merchants of 
Canton, Hongkong and clsewhere united to 
assume charge of the undertaking, by pro- 
viding the capital sum required for the ex- 
tension of the track fram Samshui to Han- 


‘-kow. It has generally, been observed that 


the construction of a railway line in any 
country. is hedged about with all sorts of un- 
expected difficulties, There' are owners of 
property and impatient shareholders 1o be 
concitiated ; there aré employés to be select: 
ed with caution, watched like a hawk, and 
won. over to’ believe, that they are not 
merely working for the benefit. of pos- 
tertty but ore themselves reaping and 
likely to renp advantages of which they 
had never dreamt. There are the natural 
difficulties of inundations, subsidences and 
what not; and there is, above all, that fre- 
quently irritating apparition in - Chinese 
territory known as fengshui, The Canton: 
Hankow Railway has experienced one and 
all of these difficulties and several others in 
addition, It has passed through a greater 
variety of vicissitudesthan could berecited off 
hand, but it has survived, which shows the cs- 
sential importanceand valucof the line. .Only 
a railway endowed with inherent merits and 
thoroughly utilitarian even to the prejudiced 
eye could have surmounted the obstacles in 
front of tho Canton-Hankow Railway, It is.

on 


ana 


yh 
MAIL 


doubtful, however, whether tire last ‘of the. 
troubles arising out of the construction works. 
has even’ yet been’ met. . From statements 
which appear in-another column, it will be 
recognised that His Excellency Sir Matthew 
Nathan has been requiring the Canton Vice- 
roy to justify his.attitude with respect to the 
proposed appointment of a Belgian cngincer 
j to supervise and conduct the completion of 
the linc. “His Excellency the Governor has 
| proceeded along the usual diplomatic course, 
‘ and has now arrived at the dead wall which 
the astute Viceroy generally manages to 
erect by hook or by crook. At a first glance 
the matter may’appear trivial ; the appoint-. 
sment | of. an cngineer, even if. he. be a 
Belgian, does not shake nations or cause 
dynasties to totter. But: it is plain that 
Sir Matther Nathan attaches, much im- 
-portance to the questions ‘Through 
H.B.M. Consul-General at Canton His 
Excellency has pointed out to the Viceroy 
that under the terms of the Britis: loan. by 
which China was enabled to secure the 
redemption of the Canton-Hankow Railway 
from foreign concessionaires, China, has | 
agreed that, in the event of foreigners being 
called in to give the benefit of their advice, | 
she will recognise Great Britain’s claini to 
first consideration. In other words, that no 
foreigner will be appoiuted uniess it be found 
that no Englishman is prepared to offer’ his | 
servites or is of sufficient ability to, fill the 
post. ‘Phe'Vicerey fias replied rather more. 


with: the con- | 


promptly than usual, but still with his tongue 
in his cheek. He has.no ‘power to deal with’ 
the matter, The Rajlway is in the hands of 
the merchants. ‘They are responsible for its 
management and its progress. If they think 
it desirable and proper to engage a Belgian’ 
engineer, well and good. He cannot inter- 
fere; he would be acting’ aéfra orres ; and it 

is imposable to say what his Imperial master 
or mistress would think of such conduct, 

Theredhe matter stands ; but it will be strange 
if Sie Matthew Nathan accepts that answer as 
final, ‘There is a large principle involved ; 
‘the Chinese made a solemn undertaking 

-which they would now repudiate. ‘The next 

step will probably be to influence Peking 

through the Foreign Office. What will interest 

most peopleis thé fact that Sir Mathew 

Nathan snould be engaged in Foreign Office 

work orat least request the Consul-eneral 

at Canton to make representations on this 

F question of the appointment. of a foreign ‘en- 

gincer. His Lexcellency’s duties are underthe 

direction of the Earl of Elgin.not Sir Edward 

Grey, It does occur to the mind’ thal His 
Iexcellency may have been charged with all 

railway oratters in South. China. He has 

certainly been indefatigable in urging forward 

the Kowloun-Canton Railway, although there 
jis stated to bea hitch in the" machinery” 
uythe New Territories at present... And it 
is not-unreasonable to suppose that progress 
in -connection with the © Canton-Hankow 
Railway.is regarded as in a way’ essential to. 
the ullimate success of the line which will 

Have its terminus at Kowloon. Atal events, 
it is the Governor of Hongkong who has 
hen in communication with the Viceroy 
of Canton: over a question which is of 
importance from the diplomatic standpoint. 
And all the principal Chinese papers in 

Canton and Hangkgng aré in, possession of 
the Viceray’s reply, which was published for 
the delectatian of their readers this morning ; 
while-everyone interested in railway affairs 
in the capital, of South ‘China knew of His 

Excellency the Governor's intervention two 
days ago... It is a curious situation and one 
that is open to explanations. The Belgian 
engineer has riot yet been engaged, so far 
as we’ know, but!the work of completing 
the line to Hankow is once again under 
weigh, and for that there is room for every 
satisfaction. Problems of procedure and 
diplomatic exchanges may occur every day 
so long ‘as the railway 1s pushed forward 
with all speed, for afler all that is the main 
thing which will give an immense impetus 
to trade in South*China and redound to the 
ultimate advantage of Hongkong. 


Witsd WIPER OUTRAGES.. 
ee = 7 i 
_ Surely it is high-tinle: that the might and 


tionals and foster her trade, should be de- 
monstrated to the audacious, gangs of blood- 
thirsty villains who infest the West River, 
It is only a month ago to-day since the as 
nam was attacked by pirates’and murder. 
commilted. The citizens of Hongkong pro-. 
tested in the most yigorous manner at the 
apparent impotency of the Government in 
dealing with the matter. In measured but 
effective language representatives of the 
leading shipping and business houses in the 
Colony expressed their views that the Sai- 
nam affair was the crowning crime in the 
calendar of West Riyer piracies. It almost 
seemed for a few days that the British 
Government would be coerced into a laggard 
recognition of the rights of British subjects 
in the Far East, espe ‘ally when our German 
friends sent a telegram to Berlin strongly 
urging upon the German Government the 
desirability of supporting the action of Great. 
Britain in any form of reprisals. that might 
be deemed proper. There have been the 
usual pour-parlers and nothing has been 
done. Now, once again, we have been 
startled by the telegram, which was publish: 
ed by the Hanghong. Jelegraph on Saturday 
afternoon, stating that a British Jaunch was. 
attacked by pirates, ore of the guard shot 
dead and four injured. That is surely a pretty 
commentary onthe protection afforded to Bri- 
tish traders inthe Fur East. The daring mar- 
auders of the West River must be laughing: 
in their sleeves at the veayels which are sup- 
posed to represent the British flag. They 
ignored H.M.S. Afocorken, boldly cane 5 
Wuchow in broad daylight,’ duccessfully 
landed their booty and quite as successfully

then, unless. we'are greatly mistaken, Great 
Britain will receive a lesson in swift venge- 
ance and the best methods of securing per- 
manent peace and security. . 


ALLHGED JOURNALISM LN 
HONGKONG, *" 
—__»—___ 

(14th August.) ‘ 
Probably the most despicable act in the 
zode of honest and -straightforward journal- 
.ism is the unwarranted and unacknowledg- 
ed appropriation of news which, belongs ‘by 
special prescription to another organ, and 
when that grossly unfair “ cribbing” occurs 
on the part of a newspaper claiming to circu- 
late within the area cévered by its better- |, 
informed leader and more vigorous ‘contem- 
porary , is not merely disgraceful—it is con- 
temptible. But surely the climax has arrived 
when our junior contemporary, the S. Ci: 
Morugag Lost, with that amazing effronter 
whiéh'has. marked certain of its isstes, bold 
appropriates a full half column of special 
intelligence (including the translation of a 
Chinese document)-which was published in 
the Hongkong Zelegraph alone ; ‘and not 
merely: refuses to give unto Cesar the things 
that aie-‘Coesar’s but actually crows over its 
suceess ns .a news-purveyor, Yet that, is 
what occurred to-day. No doubt few of our 
readets would think it. worth while to refer 
to our contemporary’ after perusing the 
Hongkong telegraph of the previous’ even: | 
ing, but for once we may just indicate 
the sort of pabulum which’ is being served 
out to those who’ patronise the - fost 
as being fresh, clever and .a testimony 
to the ubiquity of the oss correspon- 
dents, Yesterday we .reported. how His 
Excellency the Governor had requested the 
Constl:General at Canton to approach Vice- 
roy“Shum, with reference to the proposed 
appoidtment of a Belgian engineer to super- 
intend the construttion of the Canton; Han- 
kow Railway, and to point out that this 
would be a violation of the terms under 
which the British Government sanctioned 
the loan to enable the Chinese to redeem 
thedine from foreign conceisionaires. We 
received the terms of that despatch on- 
Saturday, but for reasons which we cannot 
state at present, withheld its publication 
for the time being. On. Monday morning 
several Chinese vernacular papers printed a 
despatch from the Viceroy to the British 
Consul-General at anton, declaring that’ 
the Company,, being a commercial enter- 
prise, had the ‘right to manage its own 
affairs without outside interference, Not 
a single native paper to our knowledge 
published the ‘terms: of Sir Matthew 
Nathan's despatch to the Consul-General,. 
yet the fost was able, this morning to 
print that “request”; and, “not only so, 
bat the text of its -allegedecommunication 
was identical in Janguage to that which 
appeared in the Hongkong Velegraph, \ast 
night,’ But os if that were not sufficient 
for the voracity of our shark-like contem- 
porary, it went on to givea translation, which, 
we had secured after much-trouble, of the 
Viceroy'sdetpatch. It disdained apparently | 
to take the trouble of finding out whether 
our translation was correct or not, It lifted 
the translation bodily out of our columns, 
Such compliments to our energy and accu- 
racy we do not desire. It might have been 
possible—though we doubt it, out let us be 
generous—it might have been just possible. 
for the os to have obtained, by some means 
or other, a translation of the Chung Awok 
‘o'sarticle, but does anybody pretend for. 
one single moment that the translation would 
have corresponded word for word with that 
which appearedin the Hongkong Telegraph? 
Such an idea is absurd. We expressly em- 
ployed in the translation certain phrases 
whith mose properly expressed the precise: 
meaning of the Viceroy than if we had used 
the haphezard terms.of the ordinary transla-

NANNING OPEN. TO FOREIGN 

TRAVE, i 
et 

(15th. August.) 


The news that the Chinese Government 
has acquiesced in the proposal submitted by 
Viceroy Shum that Nanning should be de- 
clared open to forcign trade—as reported by 
our’ Canton correspondent to-day— will 
arouse interest and speculdtion—interest as 
to the probable effects which will flow from 
this concession to diplomatic pressure, and 
speculation as to the motives which led 
Viceroy Shum to submit the proposal and 


Government that his suggestion should be 
adopted. At the first glance it seems pro- 
bable that British interests will be furthered 
hy the opening of Nanning, but the question 
temains whether, -after all, the resulting 
trade, in the existing conditions of river 
navigation, will be commensurate with the 
high expectations ‘held in some commercial 
quarters. . It should be explained that Nan- 
ning lies on'the northern bank of the Tso- 
chiang branch of the West River, 319 miles 
from Wuchow. It is declared to be healthy, 
and to have a population of about 100,000. 
Some, years ago, in. 1898, Mr. G. W. 
Sheppard, of Messrs. Jardine, Matheson. 
& Co., visited Nanning and recorded his 
impressions of that city, which ranks second 
to Wuchow in the province of .Kwanggi. 
He remarked that the “city has rather a 
prepossessing appearance, and the inclina-. 
tion is rather to over-estimate its importance 
after the other places e#, roufe.”” Adding: 
"A fait quantity of shipping lined the bank.” 
‘Writing in 1899, he expatiated on- the 
difficult’ navigable conditions along the 
first forty miles alter leaving Wuchow; speak- 
ing of some bau reefs of rocks." Thence 
there was a clear. run of 120 miles to Kwei- 
yuen, about half way to Nanning, but from: 
Kweiyuen “there are, tocky rapids and 
shallows extending for some miles wilh ‘very 
sharp turns in the channel, ‘which would 
sender, so it was reported, the passage of a 
steamer impassable when the river had fallen 
toa ceriain stage. ‘Some ten miles below 
Naoning, and fifty miles below it again, 
there are also two places where in low water 
junks are compelled to lighten to two and a 
naif feet." ‘That is not a cheerful prospect 
for those who entertain the idea of inau- 
gurating a river service between Wuchow and 
Nanning. In 1901, the British gunboat 
Sandpiper drawing 2 fect 6 inches ascended 
to Nanning and had rather an adventurous 
time among the many swirls and eddies, the 
channel being both tortuous and narrow, 
and, indeed, “a large swirl on one oc- 
casion suddenly rose and caused the vessel 
to-so heel that it seemed impossible 
it should recover and escape the 
rocks.” Mr, Ernest Alabaster, acting Com- 


report on the trade of Wuchow, remarked 
in rgor that “while the navigable condi- 
tions to Nanning are an obstacle yet, per- 
haps, if particular rocks be removed (no 
very difficult operation) and ‘the’ channel 
straightened, probably a special class - of 
lighter, of large carFying ‘capacity, for towing 
purposes, will be constructed and cargo ‘go 
just as- well’ by the West River.” ‘The 
same writer, looking ahead to the time 
when the ‘Canton-Hankow railway is an 
accomplished fact, says that Nanning 
would probably seriously affect. the 
trade of Wuchow. ‘“ Most ot ie lighter 
products of the fertile region” of Central 
Kwangsi will pass to Nanning for water 
carriage, but heavier products. such -as those 
ofthe mines, may be sent: here.” In sum- 
ming up the ‘acting ‘Commissioner main- 
tained ‘that’ {{ the river, werg -gleared of 


‘particularly great. 


thing like moving Wuchow ‘to Nanning.” 
Men of shipping experi¢nce in Hongkong. 
are not exactly sanguine (hat any very. great: 
impulse will be given to trade from this port ” 
by the concession granted’ by the Chinese 


}Governament. Indeed, they are-rather in, 


clined to’ doubt..whcther anything will’ be . 
yained.by the gracious offering of tte Im- 


‘perial Government. There can be no doubt 


that'as things stand atypresent, the induce, 
ment to construct speblal boats’ to pass 
the shallows ‘with sufficient power to: 
countéract the effects of the rapids is not 
It is “true ‘that’ steam * 
launches of Jight draught would” effect a. 
valuable saving of time-in the transportation 
of goods from Nanning to Wuchow, but the 
Chinese traders are seldom’ inclined to: 
balance rapidity of transit and greater cost of: 
‘earriage in favour of old-fashioned methods. 
‘of transportation, lengthy, delays and small 
‘freight charges... A matter ofa few-days is of | 
little consequence in theiy eyes' provided the’ - 
goods eventually reach their destination at 
‘the least possible ‘cost, No doubt the in-° 
troduction of railways andthe adoption of 
modern‘ ideas generally. will educate the 
better class: :of dealers to a‘ realisation 
of the value of time, but steamboat 
companics cannot, afford ,t0 pay the ex- 
penses attendant on such education of the: 
natives. Even as itis, the steamboats 
plying on.the West River barely return in- 
terest.on their capital cost’ and working’ 
expenses, One of the notable points in the 
specch of the chairman of the Hongkong,- 
Vanton and Macao Steamboat Co. at -the, 
annual meeting, yesterday’ was. to ‘the: 
effect. that "the. working of the West 
River service continues to be.'very um 
profitable,” and: the Hongkong-Kongmoon . 
‘service has had to be abandoned on that 
account, Cf course, it may be deemed 
a healthy, sign of China's, progress that she: 
should agree to the constitution of Nanning. 
as‘an open port, but was there no _a@rriere | 
fenste when the Chinese Government acced-. 
ed to the suggestion of the versatile and.-. 
astule Viceroy Shum? It must be remem: 
bered that the Chinese authorities have for 
long: viewed with anxiety-the progress of 
France: in Tonkin, and'the effects of the’ 
extefision of the Hanoi railway to: Yuonan.:' 
May it not be‘ suggested that the Chinese: 
‘Government, taking time by the forelock, have 
decitied to check French aspirations -in‘the. - 
direction of holding a'preponderating: in- 
fluence. over the. district ‘commanded by 
Nanning by setting all the Powers:at' each - 
other's throats in the contention for trado’ 
advantages in that district?. Chinese dip- 
lomacy was ever subtle in its motives; and’ 
the Chinese would be playing an -old “game: 

if their idea in opening Nanning to all ‘coun-' 
tries was-intended tp settle France's “ goose.” 
Still, looked at dispassionately, that seems 

to be the underlying object of China’s 
generosity. France may advance to Nanning 
but no farther, for then she would:have to — 
override the rights held equally by all 
nations, and the Powers would not look on’ 
calmly while France exerted her sovereign ' +: 
will over the ‘Nanning district! But the 
Chinese. are likely.to be themselves outwit- 
ted if these considerations’ actuated their 
‘policy of the open door on the West River, . 
for nothing can arrest the increasing influence 
of France along the borders of Tonkin. 
bounded by Kwangsi, and Yunnan.. The 
railway is an invaluable pioneer in interna- 
tional affairs, and France must predominate 
to the extént, at the very least, of her railway. 
extensions, It is unlikely, however, ‘that 
Nanning will become a bone of contention 
among the Powers. So long as Wuchow: 
maintains its precedence as a port and trade. 
centre in Kwangsi, Nanning ‘must.continu: 
in a subordinate position. . From all this it: 
will be seen that British interests are not. 
likely to be interféred with for years to come,” 
and itis just possible, though it is scarcely 
likely that- anyone will rely upon the event, 
that the opening of Nanning to foreign 
trade may bring additional. grist to the 
Hongkong mill, weg PER ‘

Of their own lives; their frail craft was nearly 
capsized half a.dozen times ; they kept their’ 
boat headed for the capsized sampan whose 
former oécupants were struggling in the 
-waves at the last gasp; finally, they managed 
fo drag three persone inta the lifeboat, and. 
then had to dive under the hood of the up. — 
turned sampan in order (o'rescuc two child: 
ren. Not once or twice but half a dozen 
times ‘the gallant Chinese ‘endangered sheir 
own lives and it was only when at the point - 
of exhaustion they that succeedéd'in rescu, . 
ing two helpless infants,’ ‘To.those whio have 
lived inthe East it reads: like a romance,

“Viceroy Shum's reply to despatch sent by 
British Conaul-General, Canton, relative to 
employment of foreign engineers in (he Canton- 
Hankow Railway. [t is reported that Viceroy 
Shum'sent the following reply :— 

“Replying to the British Consul-General's 
despatch contaiving a statement from the Gov- 
ernor of Hongkong to the. effect that be has 
seen in certain of the Chinese daily papers an 
“accourt of the -Canton+Hankow Railway's in- 
tention to employ Belgian engineers in the 
construction of this railway, the Viceroy de- 
sires to state that the Canton-Hankow Railway 
having received Imperial.sanction to be under 
mercantile management the employment of 
men and the management of all money affairs |. 
are al! under the merchants. As to the Rail- 
way Company employing engineers, the power. 
Js vested -in -the Company 5 the Viceroy has 
nothing to do with it; therefore, the Viceru 
requests that the above facts may be communi-» 
cated to theGovernor of Hongkong,'”

A REMARKABLE, CITY: CHAU-CHU-FU, municipal officials elsewhere in China. 

. Another detour brought us to the literary 
examination hall where formerly contests for 
the first degree were held. This also was 
clean avd in bettér repair than any examina- 
tion-hall we have seen elsewhere, and it is so 
arranged that it could well serve as a place for 
large public meetings and need not suffer the. 
destrucling fate which bas come upon the see 
cond degree hall in Canton and elsewhere. 

Instead of the rows of separate atalls which 
characterize the second degree hall, there were 
long heavy granite desks and benches under 
one large roof, Each.desk bore dividing lines 
showing that it was meant to accommodate four 
person, and the total capacity was readily esti- 
matedat 2,300, Asan examination hall oftheald 
type it will, of course, not be used further—for 
already in accordance with the new regime the 
schools in Chau-chu-fu have inken on a modern 
form and are well attended. 

Chau-chu-fu ‘harbours three. ‘missions—the 
French Catholic, the American Paptist, and 
the English Presbyterian. It was under the 
auspices of the hospitable surgeon it charge of 
the medical work of the last named mission 
that we learned as much of this interesting city 
as we did in thé brief stay -we made, te 

Although a day and fn halfenight were requit- 
-ed for. the upward trip fram Swatow, the down- |’ 
ward run was made. from 9 pm, to 7 am 
puiting us back in Swatow just in time to catch 

‘ comfortably the outgoing: steamer for Amoy— 
had she been going on time, which needless to 
say to those who have travelled, the Chinn 
coaat she did not do. ; ‘ 


mercial undert king with’ national patriotism. 
Commencement was made to-day with the 
building of the Sai Chun Nam On section of 
the Yueh Han Railway. Mr. Chang Kun 
Ying, the chief manager of the company, was 
present, and he himself, with a shovel, tuned’ 
the first-sod, in order to stimulate the workmen 
with their work, which -it will be the es- 
deavour ot the company to push on, vigor- 
ously to completion, The company has talled 
for tenders for the supply of materials, within 
thirty days.. The name of the successful ten- 
derer has not yet been disclosed. Judging 
from the ardour and enthu.iasm with which the 
work is inaugurated, under Chinese auspices, it 
is confidently expected that the date of com- 
pletion will not be far off. .

couple of hours before the residents, who have 
come down there for a little respite over night, 
will arine, one passes through a‘narrow ‘strait 
Suto a larger estuary, On the north side of 
the strait the ordinary waterfront of a Chinese 
coast city is seen, with its Customs landing, 
mission compounds, and modern godowns-pro- 
minent in the foreground. The Customs fore- 
shore is‘a large open common, ‘which, though 
unadorned, affords a veritable boon on a sum- 
mor's evening to Inrge numbers of natives who 
‘crowd there from the'r close city quarters to 
catch a refreshing sea breeze. Directly op- 
posite on the south the attractive and comfort- 
able residences of the foreign friends of China 
appear, built, or rather in somecases “perched,” 
here and there along the rockiest shore-ridge 
one has yet encountered in -China. These 
dark aud brownish granite crags in some p'aces 
assume most’ curious forms, many of the 
houses are half hidden by others atid by the 
“trees which relieve, to some extent, the rugged 
character of the hills; “and-as~seen-across-(he 
clear water dotted by many sailing sampans 
and a good baker's dozen of fair-sized merchant 
ships of various nations, siding quietly atan. 
chor, or turning slowly ina huge arc toward 
- one of the hulks moored along the city's front, 
the whole picture gives an impression of beau- 
RA long remembered, While ‘the southern 
shore is.by far the more picturesque and the 
northern flat and rather dredrand sandy, yet the 
latter enjoys, during the summer months, a 
Bearly contiquous breeze which at about one-or 
two in the afternoon generally stiffens consider- 
ably, and bears the significant name of “the 
Bwatow Doctor”—~old residents declaring its 
efficiency, and, with considerable: truth no 
Goubt, to be superior to a host of real and. wise 
aoctors. : e

wo understand that some forty years ago it was 
byt a small fishing village and has réached its 
present importance solely as a result of foreign 
trade, or at least trade carried in foreign bottoms

‘ An easly morning start gave us a’ chance to 
climb onc of ‘the highest hills-on the enst side 
“af.the river opposite the city without unneces- 
sary fatigue, and.as the ‘niists lifted and the 
horizon broadened one saw that the city yas 
centred in the midst of a striking amphitheatre 
ofbills, a full three quarters of a circle, the 
only apparent opening being toward the south 
which was the way we had ‘entered in the 
night. : 
<The second marked feature in the landscape 
isthe peculiar old bridgeacross the Han. which 
al this point is perhaps half a mile wide én fc/o. 
This consists of twenty-two piers (originally 
twenty-four) which are large enough to bear 
on-their-tops..on_either side of the. bridge's | 
(readway fair-sized shops of different sorls—bar- 
bers, blacksmiths, carpenters, etc, These piers 
are joined by-three huge slabs of pinkish 
granite, laid side by side, which by actual 
measurement are-four feet thick, four feet wide 
and forty-five feet long, and in some cases 
Innger, They were evidently put inte position 
by betng floated-on boats at highest water of 
the year and then dropped into place as the 
river level sank. In some cases they have not 
sustained their own weight and now heavy 
timbers here an‘ there do duty instead—and 
yet as a whole the bridge is quite well pre- 
served for, doubtless, it is of considerable age., 
There is a break in the bridge, however, near 
the western shore and this gap of some 150 
feet is covered by eighteen chained boats and 
plankways. At the time of high flood tide 
these boats rise to the level'of the bridge floor 
and the chains let go, whereon a feward of

[From Our. Own Corresponden 4] 


‘ iG Cantdn, 14th August, 
On the recommendation. of Viceroy Shum; 
the Imperial Government, has agreed that 
Nanning should. be declared a port open to 
the trade of the world.” Viceroy Shum tas 
" ordered tha Taotal of Chekiang and Mr. E. von 
_ Straach, the Commissioner of Customs at Wu- 
chow, to..make -all necessary arrangements 
pending the opening of Nanning asa treaty 
port. | aa on 
Thesiver between Wuchow and Nanning is 
very shallow, onlyabout three feet of water bein 
‘found in some parts. It can only be navigated 
by vessels of light draught which would have 
_ 1 to be specially constructed if a steamboat trade 
were to be inaugurated between Nanning and 
Wuchow. There-is comparatively little trade 
from Nanning at preseat, merchandise being 
brought by junks down to Wuchow,; and the 
navigation of the river is attended with consi: 
derabie difficulty, as bas been experienced by 
travellers who have passed the ripids. When 
the goods reach Wuchow they are trana-shipped 
to the regular river steamers.. As far ‘as 
«TI cao gather, it would be necessary lo con- 
struct vessels of the size of steam launches, 
and specially designed to negotiate the rapids 
by the provision of special steering’ gear, be- 
fore trade could be successfully carried onibe: 
tween the two ports, Pa 
The exact extent of the volume of trade has, 
not been. ascertained, but from the statements 
of those who were connected with a vessel, 
owned by a firm in Hongkong, running be. 
tween Wuchow and Nanning, the trade hardly 
justified the continuynce of the service.’ The 
opening of the port: might havethe effect of 
indirectly favouring. British interests, since all 
facilities granted to trade are calculated to in- 
fluence the British trade in South China, The 
‘ ‘opening of Nanning may also affect French 
interests in the north.

REMARKABLE BXAMPLE OF SELF-HELP.” 


: ; : ‘rgth-inst., 
Friends of Mr. Kwan King Sun, a younger 
brother of Dr. Kwan K ng Leung who js‘ 
well-known resident of this Colony, will ba 
pleased to know that he has gmduated this 
Year from the Cornell University, Ithaca, N.Y., 
with honour, and has been elected a member 
of the American Chemical Society. This is a 
remarkable record fora Chinese student abroad 
scoking for an higher and advanced education, 
since this young man only went over to the 
United States in July Inet year, In the space 
of one college year, he has made himself pro. 
Gcient in all requirements. of the college, pass 
‘ed his final examinations and receivin his ai. 
picma with honour, which, it is,stated, would 
have taken an ordinary man three years to 
achieve, ve a, Mee a 

A brief account of Mr. Kwan's career may 
|hot be saueresliag. to the numerous ac: 
‘guaintances of Dr. Kwan King: Leung, 
‘Kwan ‘King Sun received his first Eoglish edu. 
cation in the Diocesan School in this Colony, 
Thence ho-proceeded to Tichtsin and entered 
the  Tientsin University where he graduated 


Mr, | 


with distinction juat prior to the outbreak of‘ 


the Boxer trouble, Being then eager to obtain 
a higher education abroad, but belng in hum- 
ble circumstances, he resolutely set himuelf to 
secure employment asa mining engineerin = 
Certaintin minoin the Straits Settlements, There 


he remained two years and during thittime he ‘ - : 


hot only won for himselfthe reputation of 


being a faithful, conscientious, and efficient 
worker, ‘but also the high esteem and regard 
ofhis.employer, In deference to his father’s: 
wishes he again made his way back to Tientsin 
where his two other brothers are in the Govern: 
ment service, and joined the mercantile firm of 


Messrs. Billfetd. and Bon, For, four yearshe 


worked hard both during and after office hotirs, 
When‘ he bad saved up sufficient means for 
his support and ‘education, he resigned bis 


position and proceeded to the States where he | 


haa nop distinguished himself, We under 


stand that Mr, Kwan intends further to equip’ 


himbelf in other branches of science.

The Indias policeman, who was temanded 
on the! 8theinstant by Mr. F. A. Hareland in 
order that he- might try. to re-arresl a prisoner 
whom he allowed to escape, came.up.on re. 
mand, at the Magistracy, to-day. The police- 
‘man failed to effect acapture, It may.be ree 
mom berad thaton the morning of the 7th instant 
his Wofship convicted a coolie for theft and 
sentenced him to (bree woeks' hard labour and 
six hours’ stocks, The defendant was deputed 
by the inspector-on-duty to see that the stocks 
sentence was carried out, The Indian took 
the conv'cted man down to the Praya C entral, 
saw that ho was properly locked in the stocks 


and stood gnard over him. When the cooclie 


had done three hours in the stocks he turned | 


40 the Indian conatable and said that he wanted 
to’ goto.the public latrine, The 
xotthe man out ofthe stocks, went a bit further 
by releasing one hand of the coolie's from the 
handcuffs and marched him to the latring, The 
coolio.entered, while the policeman waited at 


the entters door, He waited for a considerable - 


length of time and finding that there were no 


'| signs of the coolie he went in to investigate; 


olicoman 


‘when to his surprise he discovered the latring . 


was tmply. His man had escaped: through: 
the weatern door of the lauine, taking with him 
a pair of Government handcuffs, His Worship 
held thal accused had neglected his duty, but 
as he had « very good character in the force 
he would take that into consideration and deal 


with him leniently. He would pay a fine of $7, |

BANITARY INSPFCTOR ARRESTED, . 
, 
%, ; Oth inst. 

‘Quite a sensation wis caused in certain 
circles in the Colony $43t night when it became 
known that a European sanitary inspector had 
been arrested by virtue of a warrant, While 
outsiders were wondering who the man could 
be and what fed to’ his arrest, the’ subject of 
vtheir conversation was being, entered in the 
charge-sheet by Inspector Ritclue, _ After this 
fad been done and other formalities gone 
through the sanitary inspector was taken toa 
cell to pass the night. : ; ; 

This moming, the inspector, whose name 18 
Francis Ward, and who was a member of the 
local police farce three years ago, before being 
‘transferred to the Hanitary Department, was 
brought before Mr. F. A. Hazeland, at the 
Polica Court, ta plead to the charge. _ The

ceptacles, . He was not there long, The build-

fo have, an animus againstthe drainage men,

RAPH - SATURDAY, AUGUST +i 


PIRACY IN CHINA. 


VIEWS OF A WEST RIVER TRAVELLER. 

Whilst_ the tragedy on the. West: Rive’ 
steamer Sainam stands out in clear outline in 
the minds cf our reiders and before its main 
fentures become blurred and indistinct or lese ' 
their hold an the meniory, it may bs of advan- 
tage to pl.ce the dire event io its historical 
setting, giving to the. piratical raid con- 
sideration in telation (o Chinese social fife on, 
the one hand and to foreign intercourse witl 
China on the other. ae 

The first knowledge which the West had uf | 
the East on the Kwangtung co ine and 
beyond isin part knowledge of pirates, and 
their evil doings. Portuguese prowess, at a 
time when the maritime enterprise af Portuga! | 
gave her the lead in these seas, was directed to 
the patting down of pirncy. ‘By aiding China to 
sweep the waterways clear of these pests to civil- 
ization Portugal made good herfoating in Macao. 
It should not be forgotten or overlooked that 
from the first days of foreign settlement in South 
China, ‘such cecupation has been rendered 

assible by the intervention of Western nation 
in policing the waterways’ needéd' for their 
traffic with this great Empire of the East. 

- CHINESE BENEFITS,” 

Among the chief benefits to China arising 
out of the British occupation of Hongkong, 
was-the suppression of piracy in the waters of 
the Colony. - The first Chinese r:cords of 
Hongkong are said to date from the thirteenth 
‘century. Phe island was then a_ piratical 
stronghold, and despite tha efforts made from 
time to ime, with varying success to destroy: 
or drive out sea-fobbers, the story of early 
decades and centuries is -largely one of 
ineptitude on .the part of the Government 
and of open defiance of law and order on the 
part of piratical hordes making this island their 
headquarters, ‘There is evidence that the 
aystem of blackmail levied, ou craft passing 
through what is now the harbour of Hongkong 
was complete, and exactions were rigidly en- 
forced, The island was a pirates’ island; the 
higher elevations of the Peak district served 
as lookout stations and no boat ‘that ap. | 
proached was permitted to leave the island till 
toll was taken. ; 


HEREDITARY PIRATES, nN 


That piracy which has become hereditary 
among the worst classes of seafaring people is 
not easy to check, still Jess to stamp out, is 
witnessed by the records of the Supreme Court 
during the ensliest years of British rule. “This 
form of crime threatened to imperil com- 
mercial enterprise and “td*rethrd the develop- 
ment ol the new Colony. [t was, indeed, a. 
formidable factor’ in a situation that seemed. 
beyond the resources of law and order to 
cope with, That a betier time came when’ 
life and property under the British flag 
was made and remains sacred whéther a- 
shore or afloat ig due to the patient and per- 
sistent endeavour to bring the ‘administration 
of British Jaw in Hongkong into line with the 
administration of British law throughout the 
world. [n China many things remain as they 
were in spite of Western impact, the object 
lessons afforded by Western civilization,’ and 
the pressure brought to bear by Western 
diplomacy. Among the bad things that abide’ 


is riverine piracy. : 
‘ “ROBBERY UNDER ARMS.” . 


. The Kwangtung province is: a network of 
navigable rivers and streams, On its water- 
ways is borne meschandise of value to arouse 
the cupidity and to challenge the daring of des- 
peradoes—akin spirit to those -who infested 
-the waterways of Hongkong and Macao. 

To have travelled occasionally on the rivers 
uf South China is to have heard stories told" 
by boatmen and native fellow-passengers, ‘of 
robberies under arms accompanied often by. 
by deeds af violence that make the blond ran} 
cold. Frequent orreguldr journeys in passage 
boats.or in house boats hired by the day or 
month almost invariably bring the non- 
Chinese traveller into contact sooner or later 
with robbers. Ii is safe to affirm that of the 
many missionaries whose work takes them 
at stated times into the interior of Kwang: 
tong there ‘are ‘few who cannot tell of 
adventures with robbers and pirates end- 
ing usually in the loss of money and bng- 
age. In the safest limes there are always 
“unsafe districts and under a weak regimé 


| whether of high provincial or local authority 


few districts can be accounted safe. Condi- 
tions on the rivers have their parallel on shore. 
Robber bands scour the country attacking and 
raiding the villnges.- Should resistance be 
offered it is met by the use of firearms and 
other weapons. Where villages and hamlets on 
a wide country side unite for mutual protection 
they may and often do succeed in keeping 
robbers at a distance. The alternative is to 
submit. to blackmail collected as the price of 
immunity from pillage. Wide regions of 
country are wholly unsafe after darkness has 
fallen and in these places no: wise_man_will go

"SOME CAUSES OF PIRACY.: 


In bad yéars when crops fail the dangers 
are greatest, Where opium smoking and gam- 
bling are rife there is generally high-handed 
robbery and other crimes of violence. This 
being an-aspect of life in the Southern pro- 
vinces, tragedies like the Safsam disaster will 
recur as non-Chinese, responsible for riverine 
navigation relax’ their vigilance. During tbe 
past thirty years piracies on the Spark, Namoa, 
and Greyhound have shown the, preparedness 
of the Chinese sea robbers to take every 
advantage of udwatchfulness. In each instance 
‘the action is ‘the same. Information is 
obtained ‘of treasure’ or cargo of special 
value fprming part of the steamet’s Joad- 
ing. A plot: is Inid to sectire the booty. | 
Robbers disguised as passengers go on board 
and mingle freely with the law-abiding.’ Other 
.members of the gang are told off to receive 
the'spoll at o given point-in junks manned fov, 
the purpose, At.a preconcerted signal the 
pirates have recourse to arms and seeing that 
the only resistance to be anticipated is from the 
foreigners on board-obyiously the first thing to 
be done isto renderimpossible such resistance. 
Methods of procedure on the Safsam bear the 
closest possiblé resemblance to those in vogue 
since the earliest successful allempts to plun- 
der steamships owned, controlled and navigated 
by n-d-Chinese * ; : 

A HINT TO SHIPOWNERS ' : 

The inference from what is written above is 
sufficiently clear, If coasting and, riverine 
steamers are to be run without the risks that 
have attended navigation on the West River, jt 
can anly be by adequate provision on the 
part of shipowners against ‘river pirates who 
embark as passengers. The Chinese Provincial 
Government may promise sufficient protection; 
ft ia not in a position to. fulfil its promises of 
to carry out its intentions however good in the 
direction desired In a reformed China, under 
a lesa corrupt administration, when crimes of 
violence puch as now prevail throughout the 
two Kwaog Provinces have become fewer, when 


‘| the strong, just hand of those who govern is 


ralsed to shield the helpless upd to punish 
wickedness and vice, sojourners from the West 
may rely .on yiceroys, governors, prefects, 
and magistrates to grant the strangers within 
their gates the needed protection [In travel. ! 
Tilithen they must look to thamselves, and 


the placing, of. a sufficient numberof: armed 
guards on every ‘river steamer will be the 
re-introduction of a practice which: twenty-five 
years ago rendered the .Canton river: boats 
‘nbsolutely safe,. Considerations of comfort. 
speed, and.economy should, from the passen- 
gers viewspoint, give: place to considerations 
of safety, and only. the sleamers that are guard- 
cd, as were the Canton boats in earlier days, 
be uted by non-Chitiese as a mean of travel) 
through dangerous waterways,— Contributed. «

“KOWLOON DIVERSIONS. 
solace balla 
WaTCHING FOR THE'UMPRELLA THIEVES, 


“ . 16th inat. 

Some fancy umbrellas appear to have taken 
-thefancy of Kowloon thieves, Umbrellas wbich 
have been placed in the hat-stand one minute 
have disappeared the next. Seyeral houses 
have becn visited in this manner and gamps 
have vanished, but the thieves could never he 
caught. ‘Special watches have been kept, but 
probably the thieves knew where danger lay 
and kept away. | Immediately the watcher is 
yone, away goes another umbrella. - “here 
is no difhculty: in the way of the um- 
brella thieves to remove what they want, 
undetected, from some houses in Kowloon, 
and even in Hongkong, (The main door of 
a house is left wide open and the hat-stand 
can be seqa from the street, The “boys” of 
the house are at the back, master at office, and 
the amisiress upstairs and the entire portion of 
the ground. floor is. deserted. «The umbrella 
thief knows that, simply by keeping the house 
he intends tu rob under observation fora few 
days, the chance will arrive when he can ie-

HONGKONG .MEAT SCANDAL. — 
DISEASED PIGS AT THE ABATTOIR, 


. 


: . i 17th inst. 
Cattle Inspector J, ‘I. Cotton, of the Kennedy 
Town Cattle Depot, charged two cooties before 
Mr. H. H, J.'Gomperiz, at the Police Court, this 
morning, with removing a dead :pig into the’ 
slaughter house for the purpose o! sing it 
for human food, Inspector Cotton said he was 
in charge of the Kennedy Town Cattle Depot. ' 
At a quarter past four yesterday afternoon he 
saw tho two defendants attempting to smuggle 
the carcass of a dead pig into the slaughter: ° 
house, -Hastopped the men and on examining 
the animat he found that it had bean dead for 
about two or three hours, Death was due to 
heat apoplexy, The flesh was feverish and high 
in colour. Continuing, the inspector sald that’ ~ 
this bas been the continual practice of coolies, 
but they were seldom caught as men ‘were ems 
ployed simply for that purpose, althcugh’ it 
cannot bo proved. The custom in vogue at’ 
the.depdt is that pigs to be killed must be con- 
fined in the swine ap for foxty-olght hoprs 
so that they can be thoroughly examined. be» + « 
fore being slaughtered, As often occurs, :pige 
die in the swine depdt belore they can be ox: 
amined, and the coolies make attempts to. 
smuggle their carcates into tho slaughtor- 
house and have the animal dressed and servo: 
instead of suffering the loss, which they woul 
have to do under ordinary circumstances, 

_ The defendants said that when they got the 

ig out of the swine depot it was alive and well, 

hen the inspector stopped them it died, 
His Worship imposed a fine of $5 each.

ven agains! Chan Cheung Chi alone, As

they are kepi on- record, But if for any

‘SENSATIONAL MORDE 
' DEEP BUY, 
Ro. 
» OCCUPANTS OF. FISHING JUNK KILLED. 
ALLEGED MURDERERS:AT MACAO. 
; oe as ., ¥9th inst, 
A highly sensational murder is :alleged to 
have-been committed by a mutinous crew ofa 
fishing junk, in the waters. of the Calony, op 
the night of the and inst, when the master of 
the junk, his wife nnd theit_son were donc to 
death... é seas 
The junk on which the tragedy occurred is a 
deep sea fishing boat and carries aicrew of 
ten persons, which’ comprised the master and 


owner of the junk, his wife, his son, daughter 


and nephew, und a crew! of five men. It is 
reported that some time back one of the men- 
bers of the crew suggested to the owner of the 
junk that he would like to make his daughter 
his wife. The master objected to the alliance 
and there the matter was supposed to have 
ended. The master forgot about the mater 
afterwards and there was nothing to show that 
the rejected suitor had any ill-feeling. That 
there was an ill-feeling, nevertheless, is alleg- 
ed, and that the alleged lover incited the re-

MEMORIAL TO THER GOVERNOR. 


‘MR. CHURCHILI'S REMARKAILE IGNORANCE, 
. b7th inst, 

The Bishop af Victorin writes us as follows :— 

Owing in partto the remarkable ignorance 
displayed by 31r, Winston Churchill with regard 
to the scandals attendant on‘the system of 
Opium Farming ; and also in part to the-actual 
scandal which has recently occurred in the 
Colony in that connection, your columns, and 
those of your contemporaries, have of late con- 
tained not intrequent notices of the present 
condition of the Opium Trade. It may there- 
fore interest your readers’ to know that the 
following petition, signed by the ministers and. 
senior missionaries.of the Iizitish Churches and 
Missions in Hongkong, was recently sent in to 
the Government. 

H.E. | be Governor has kindly promised to 
give the matier his careful consideration.—I 
am, etc. ‘ 

: J.C. Victoria, . 
8t. ‘Paul's College, 1 
17th August, 1926. | 

SIR,-At atime when the House of Com- 
mons has just passed a resolution “That this 
House reaffirms ite conviction that the Iado- 


Chinese Opium trade is morallf indefensible 
and: requests His Ma‘ ecty's Government to 
take such steps as nay be, necessary for bring. 
ing it to a speedy close,” it seems to us that it 


will not be inopportune to present o humble.|. 


address to Your Excellency, and the Goverm- 
ment,of this Colony, with reference to the 
system on which the traffic in Opium is con- 
ducted in Hongkong. : 

But before we do this-we would venture to 
call your Excetlency's attention to the striking 
consensus of opinion which appears to prevail 
now “in various past of the world with, regard 
to the use of opiuiw. }n China more than one 
of the high officials af the. Empite have dectar- 
ed against its use, and have issued or are about 
to issue, regulations to check it within their 
own jurisdictions ; and the Viceroy of Nankin 
‘has undertaken ‘to present to the Imperial 
Government'a petition against the trade in 
Opium, which is now in course of signature by 
the niissionaries at work in Chita, 

The Government of Japan | absolutely 
prohibits its use by its:‘own peogle, and has 
passed stringent laws to eradicate its use 
from amongst the Chinese resident within its 
dominions. The Governtnent of the Philip- 
pines, afieran exhausive inquiry into the sub-. 
ject conducted by a Commission specially 
appointed for the purpose, has decided to.adopt 

. with regard to the use of Opium in 
pines almost identical with those ol 
Japan fos 

J a'pass on to our own Colonies in Australia 
the Commonwealth Government, acting on the 
initiative taken by the Chinese Community, has 
isstied a proclamation, prohibiting the tmporta- 
lion of op-um into Australia, except for medical 
-use, from the rst of January, 1926. The Trans- 

ial has passed a yet more stringent meagre 
of protizpition, “ In short, on all sides there is 
evidence, of a siacere desire to prohibit or 
lo limit, so far. ag possible, the traffic in, and 
use¢ of, opium. : 

_ Yaur petitioners are earnestly, desirous that 
the relationship of the Hongkong Government 
to the traffic should be amended. — Far, in our 


opinion; the present system of Farming, whith- 


isin operation in this Colony, tends directly 
to enedurage the use of opium and for 
this encouragement the Government is respon- 
sible. The Govgrnmeat -is interested. in 
i s_much revenue ‘as possible, and 
by calling for tenders stimun rt 
on'the part of those who -bid for the right of 
{aiming to increase their bids for that tight as 
much as possible. The Opiom Farmer must 
xet his money hack, and therefore he uses 
eycry means in his power toa encourage the 
uscof apium, He pusttés his business to the 
ulmost extent. The Philippine Commissioners, 
in summing up the conclusions which it had 
formed, strongly condemns the ‘system of 

Farming far the following neason: : 

(a). The farmer endeavours to increase his 
profits by. extending Kis business, and so 
the-use of epium is Increased. 

(b) Extensive suvoggling -also exists under 

, this system as found in. those countries 
visited by the Committee. : 

(c) The matter of raising a revenue by such 
a system exposes the Government to mis- 
apprehension and detraction,. , 

(d) Itis hardly moral to delegate to an in- 


such authority in the way of supervising, 
detecting, and policing as the farmer usual- 
ly exercises: To exercise such authorily is 
a function of the Government only. 

pout of Philippine Commission p. 45.) 

Again, in discussing the conditwns of the 
traMfic in Java, where farming had been tried 
an S abandoned, as was also the casein Saigon, 
the Committee writes s— 

“The old system of farming 
sate of opium, in fact, not only was 
counteracted ‘in practice, as far as the 
gradual reduction of the. consumptien of that 
drug was concerned, but it was also a source 
of corruption and bribery of Government em: 
ployees on the part of the Chinese farmers, 
who were tempted to extend the sale of the 
drug to persons who were unauthorized to buy 
i, [t was also the cause of frequent distarb- 
ances, in cases where it was employed by un- 
principled persons as a means of revenge. 
These persons would ‘sureptniousty place 
Opiam in the houses of. private persans, and 
then malicieusly and falsely accuse them of 
possessing \-pium clandestinely. ‘The: prin- 
cipal reason for the” «bandonment of this sys- 
tem was that the former, in order to push his 
|. business, exhausted all aviilable mears to ex- 
tend the sale of his merchandise.” (ibid p. 38.) 

The experience in’ Hongkong has no been 
very dissimilar to that in Java. 


out the 


_ Your petitioners are not_ prepared to make a. 


recommendation to the Government with re- 


gard to what alternative system of dealing with’ 


the use of Opium, whether by High Tariff, or 
High License, or Prohibition, or Government 
Monopoly, (which Jast is secommended by the 
Philippine Commission), it would be best to 
adopt in Hongkong, To do so would be be- 
‘yond our province: What we do most earnest- 
ly desire is iis: that our-Government should 


:] be clean handed in this matter, and snould.use 


its great icfluence and authority to check 
rather than to encourage the, use of this in- 
junious drug. With this end in-view we would 
conclude ‘with two definite petitions to yorr 
Excellency: eG, 

at) That your Excellency will cause careful 
inquiry to be made, by Commission’ or other 
wise, as to what may. be the best method by 
which the Government may check and, if 
posible, in time entirely abolish the use of 
opiur:, except for medicinal purposes, in this 
Colony. A 

(2). That Your Excelle cy will give instruc- 
tions that ihe pupils in the public schnols of 
this Colony shall be taught the evil and debas- 
ing, results of the opium habit; and that the 
primers of hygiene, used’ as _text-books in the 
said schools, shall-include the necessary in- 
formation uf this matier, : 

With regard to this matter of edacation, we 
would beg leave toadd a quotation from the 
evelence of a Japanese Pastor,’ resident in 
Formosa, which is supported by the evidence 
of many others. oo ods an 

“ There is no one factor that more strongly 
influences the youny ‘generation against. the 
opivin vice than the instruction given them in 
the public schools regarding the poisonous and 
pernicious effects produced by the drug. The 
Chinese youth are slowly learning the Japanese 
lanjuage, and with: it are acquiring - Japanese, 
ideas and ideals. among which the idea most 
deeply inculcated is the perniciousness and 
|. disgrace of the opium vice for which they are 
‘taught to have an absosrence.” (Ibid p. 63.) 

Atthe same time, however, we would point 
obt, and we doubl not that your Excellency 
willagree with us,thatsolongasthe Government 
encourages rather than checks the opium vice, 
instruction in the schools will be of little avail. 

Therefore your W'etitioners would humbly 
pray, etc, etc, oe 

P - We have the honour to be, 

: . : Sir, 3 

Your Excellency's most obedient servants, 

J.C. VICTORIA.

A SIGNIFICANT MODIFICATION. 


— “ : 
In a Government Gacelie Extraordinary

sionsi— . . 
"Provided alwayd that inthe, event of the 
exportation of raw opitim (rom India, being so 
restricted during the term hereby granted, as 
in the opinion of the Governor-in-Council seri- 
ously to affect the business of the grantee, the 
onus of proof whereof shall rest on the grastee,- 
the Governar-in-Council upon the application 
of the grantee, shall reduce the rent. payable 
under this grant.to such an extent of may 
modify the terms of this grant in such other 
manner as to the Guvernor-in-Council may 
seem equt able, ; ae 
“Provided also thatin the event of the expor- 


tation of raw opium from India being prohibite 


ed. during the term hereby granted and ‘the 
grantee not being able to procure sufficient 
opium fer toiling the grantee may give notice 
to the. Governor-in-Counctl of his desire to 
surrender this grant, and, if, in’ the opinion of 
the Governor-in-Connsil such - prohibition 
seriously affects the business of the grantec, 
the onus of proof whereof shall rest on the 


grantee, the Governor-in-” ouncil shall ‘there-. 


upon, or so soon thereafter, as he may deem 
desirable, cancel the same. - Nothing in. the 
two preceding provisos is to be taken to 
mean that ‘an aheration in’ the quantity of- 
op.um exoorted from India is to be accepted 
‘as prim? facie evidence that.the Farmer 1s 
entitled to a reduction.of rent orto a modifica 
ion_o terms of this grant.” .

TELEGRAPHIC COMMUNICATIONS, ; 

The Viceroy, considering that trade was 
much hampered by there being no telegraphic 
communication belween Canton, Yangehiang, 
Kau Chow and Chi’ao Chou: Fu, recently wrote 
the Inspector-General of Telegraphs on the 
subject. The latter replied that, he would: at 
once give orders for the‘construction of a line’ 
‘between Canton, Yang Chiang and Cha'o 
Chou Fu, but that a line between Yang Chiang: 
and Kau Chou was at present impracticable,

PROPOSED COMMERCIAL COLLEGE, 


(From Our Own Corretpandent.] 
~, Canton, 14th August. 
Last year Viceroy. Shum suggested to the 
General Chamber of Commerce the advisabi-. 
lity of opening a commercial college, but no 
step was apparently taken to establish such an 


institution, Now a member of the gentry . 


named Sum Hau Chi comes forward and offers 


to-establish a college of this description at his. 


own expense. He applied to the Educational! 
Department for a piece of land at a site and 
the request was readily granted. Mr. Sum isa 
_ well-educated gentleman and has had a great 
deal ercial experience. “He hasob= 
tained the co-operation of a certain Mr. Chil, 
«who has completed his studies in a similar col- 
lege in Japan, ‘Mr. Chii will probably be ap- 
‘pointed the head-master. of the proposed insti+ 
tation.
